The trust page now opens a **modal inventory of all 100** (48 defects caught · 51 correct or hostile documents passed clean · 1 real bug found and fixed), rendered from a manifest only a fully verified battery run can write and pinned by a test that counts the cards. **The battery is now a CI gate**: every push rebuilds all 100 from scratch and one violated truth fails the build — every failure is a defect to fix, in a sample or in the checker. "Can I trust this?" now leads the top navigation. **No new attack surface**: trap PDFs from a dev-only script, generated-page content, one nav reorder. Tests 2,923. The verification corpus now spans **136 documents: 36 real + 100 synthetic.**


### v1.121.0 — 2026-08-28 · Every open Dependabot alert cleared (dependency safety update)

GitHub's automatic advisory watcher had three open alerts; all cleared. The high-severity one — `extract-zip`, with **no patched version in existence** — was cured by removal: **puppeteer 25**'s browser installer no longer uses it, and the package is gone from the dependency tree. **@nuxt/ui 4.11** clears the form-component advisory (components unused here), and **mermaid 11.17.2** (development-only) clears five more. Verified before release: 2,922 tests green, a live page render + axe pass on the new Chrome 152, UI pages screenshotted intact, documentation diagrams pixel-identical, and `fast-xml-parser` unmoved so document scores cannot shift. No code path that receives, sends, or stores anything changed.


### v1.120.0 — 2026-08-28 · The trap corpus reaches 50 (all held); the trust page explains Matterhorn (no new attack surface)

The adversarial corpus grows 39 → 50, **all 50 designed truths holding**: navigation twins (a 12-page document without bookmarks flagged, its bookmarked twin clean), a cover sheet that must not be punished for being small, the decoration-vs-`<Figure>` contradiction (the tag is the author's claim — flagged), rotated pages, formula twins (`/ActualText` spoken form honored), a declared language span (never penalized), a descriptive tagged link, an **integration file carrying three unrelated defects flagged together in one report**, and the **grand good twin** — headings, described figure, `/Scope`-directed table headers, a labeled tagged form field, tab order, title, language — scoring **100/A**, the proof the checker recognizes complete work. Two of the three initial failures were the *samples*, and the checker's floors won both arguments (a 48-character cover sheet is honestly textless against the 50-character real-text floor; a good twin genuinely lacked `DisplayDocTitle` — the very advisory another trap proves); the third was a test regex missing the finding's real wording. The trust page now lists **all 31 Matterhorn checkpoints** with the tandem explained — Title II/IITAA name WCAG, Matterhorn (published by the PDF Association, veraPDF's own steward) translates it into PDF-testable form — and the closer gains the strongest card: **qpdf, veraPDF, and Matterhorn are the world's tools**, used daily by professional remediators and certified specialists; one person assembled them, the world builds and checks them. Hero copy: internal and external agencies, easier than SiteImprove, clearer than PAC; verdict pills now say what each verdict is. **No new attack surface**: trap PDFs generated by a dev-only script, and generated-page content. Tests 2,922 unchanged. The verification corpus now spans **86 documents: 36 real + 50 synthetic.**


### v1.119.0 — 2026-08-28 · /trust joins the app; nav rows stop wrapping mid-label; sitemap + SEO pass (no new attack surface)

The trust page became a real route: `/trust` renders inside the site's own layout (header nav, footer, theme) instead of the static `/trust.html` that v1.118.0 introduced and never deployed. Its body remains generated — `pnpm build-brief` writes `app/data/trustBody.ts` from the same fill as the emailable `docs/brief` twins, with a test pinning that the page body appears **verbatim** inside the standalone brief; the `v-html` rendering it is the same trust class as §10's (repo-authored template plus machine-formatted numbers, nothing request-derived). The timeline on it now reads newest-first. **Nav fix**: seven footer items in a no-wrap `max-w-4xl` row were compressing labels in half ("What's / New") at laptop widths — header and footer chrome now run `max-w-6xl` with `flex-wrap` rows and `whitespace-nowrap` labels (reading content keeps `max-w-4xl`), pinned by test. **SEO**: a `sitemap.xml` regenerated by `pnpm build-brief` (honest `<lastmod>`; lists every public page and nothing `robots.txt` disallows — pinned so the two files can never argue), the `Sitemap:` line in robots.txt, the site meta description cut 217 → 151 characters, `/trust` canonical + og/twitter overrides, and a gated `preconnect` for the analytics origin — the page's only third-party contact. Hreflang reviewed and deliberately omitted (single-language site). **No new attack surface**: one Vue page rendering repo-authored generated content, CSS-only nav changes, and two static SEO files; no route handler, parameter, dependency, stored field, or retention change. Tests 2,915 → 2,922.



### v1.118.0 — 2026-08-28 · "Can I trust this?" trust page in both navs; the trap corpus doubles to 39 (no new attack surface)

Adds a plain-English trust page at **`/trust.html`**, linked as "Can I trust this?" from the header and footer navs — the manager-facing brief (Title II ADA / WCAG / IITAA framing with links to each, the veraPDF referee, the trap battery, the specialist feedback loop, the publicly-lost disputes, live usage numbers under a big date stamp). It is a **static file in `public/`**, regenerated by `pnpm build-brief` from live sources (`/status` JSON, git, package.json) and written **byte-identical** to `docs/brief/checker-brief.html` — pinned by `trustPage.test.ts`, so the site can never tell a different story than the document being emailed around; every link to it is a plain `<a>`, never NuxtLink (the twice-shipped `/status` trap, pinned again here). The docx twin is verified free of Word field codes on every build. **The adversarial corpus more than doubled, 18 → 39 samples, all 39 truths holding**: batch two adds skipped/generic/mixed/empty heading pathologies, filename-shaped and display-suppressed titles, direction-less header cells (the v1.108 dispute as a permanent fixture), fake typed bullets, nested and ragged tables, bare-URL / "click here" / untagged links, ID-less footnotes, unnamed layers, a circular RoleMap that must not hang, a remapped standard type, JavaScript actions, missing tab order, and two good twins the checker must NOT flag (`/ActualText`, `/Artifact`) — every one passed on the first run. **Reviewed for surface**: the trust page is static HTML with no scripts beyond none at all, no form, no new route handler (Nitro serves `public/` as-is), no parameter, dependency, stored field, or retention change; the generator runs only on the developer machine. Tests 2,910 → 2,915.



### v1.117.0 — 2026-08-28 · Hollow alt text counts as missing, proven by a new adversarial synthetic corpus (18/18 designed truths held)

**The question behind this release: does the checker actually work?** The real-document corpus proves self-consistency, not correctness — nobody knows a real report's ground truth to the last tag. So this release adds the opposite instrument: **18 hand-built adversarial PDFs, each constructed around one designed truth** (`pnpm synthetic-controls`, regenerating deterministically into `controls/synthetic-*.pdf` so each can also be uploaded to production by hand). The suite probes what a keyword-matcher-in-a-lab-coat would fail: a metadata-rich but untagged scan (0/F held), a structure-tree cycle (no hang), sixty nesting levels, paragraphs wearing `<H1>` tags, the Colton-shape detached decoy (ignored per v1.111.0), text painted outside any tag, hostile `<script>`/mustache strings (inert end to end), bold-text-instead-of-headings, InDesign RoleMap soup with a misspelled `/Lbody`, the Canva empty-pairs page (the v1.110.0 guard's first end-to-end fixture), an unlabeled form beside its labeled twin, and a rasterized letterhead line (v1.105.0 warning fires). **Score: 18 of 18 truths held — after the suite caught one real bug**: `/Alt (   )`, whitespace only, passed the figure census's emptiness check and counted as a real description. The formula branch has trimmed since v1.92.0; the figure branch never caught up — fixed for `/Alt` and `/ActualText` alike, and **no real control document carries hollow alt, so nothing re-grades**. Three other initial failures were the SAMPLES being wrong, and the checker's definitions won each argument (tagged image-only ≠ scanned, because alt can carry the text; a 12-paragraph one-pager is legitimately heading-less; a 371-character single `Tj` run is a fixture artifact no exporter produces — pdf.js truncates such runs at ~112 chars, worth knowing, never occurring in real documents). Also fixed: the `#config` alias never resolved under `scripts/`, which had silently broken `verify-controls.ts` since the analyzer extraction — `scripts/tsconfig.json` plus `pnpm verify-controls`/`pnpm synthetic-controls` root aliases. Full corpus now: **54 documents (36 real + 18 synthetic), zero invariant failures, zero real-document changes.** **No new attack surface**: one trim in the figure census, two dev-only scripts, a tsconfig. Tests 2,907 → 2,910.



### v1.116.0 — 2026-08-28 · Security-review follow-ups: the attribution guard covers figures and links, and quoted document text cannot flip an icon (plus corpus verification)

Closes both below-the-bar items from today's security review of v1.105.0→v1.115.0 (13 commits, 59 files; **no new vulnerabilities found** — every new PDF-quoted string traced to an escaping sink, the reachability walk bounded and function-local, `/status` additions derived from already-published numbers, `mapEntryError`'s info-leak guard intact). First: the v1.110.0 attribution guard protected only the heading census, while **figure text and tagged-link text** still read the raw marked-content map — on a page whose attribution failed, a plain photo could be called a "figure containing text" with retag advice attached, and a tagged link could carry a fragment of somebody else's sentence whose very non-emptiness suppressed the geometry fallback. Both now honor the same per-page verdict, computed once and threaded to all three consumers; the annotation's tagged status and author-given `/Alt` survive, since they come from the structure tree rather than the attribution map. Second: the neutral-icon classifier keyword-matched whole finding lines, so a document containing "advisory" in a heading could turn its own quoted failure line into a neutral bullet — display-only, self-inflicted-only, and fixed by stripping balanced quoted spans before matching plus sanitizing sample quotes at the source, so the classifier judges the report's wording and never the document's. Also in this release: the Matterhorn **"Issues found" chip is red** (green good, red bad — amber did not read as the bad one against emerald; the `veraPDF` provenance tag stays amber). **Corpus verification ran end to end: 36 control documents, zero invariant failures** — grade always equals what its score implies, every weight matches its format's own authored table (each sums to exactly 1.0), severity caps hold, the legacy `.xls` refuses as designed, and a before/after sweep shows **no control moved** on any of this release's changes. Dependency snapshot: two advisories, both unreachable (`@nuxt/ui` form components unused and no credentials exist; `extract-zip` install-time only, no patch published). **No new attack surface**: two reliability gates on existing censuses, one classifier hardening, one CSS class. Tests 2,897 → 2,907.



### v1.115.0 — 2026-08-28 · The Matterhorn checklist is one column, with display-size checkpoint numbers (layout only)

Third and final pass on the panel, closing what the previous two left behind. v1.113.0 gave checkpoints WITH findings the full row and v1.114.0 banded alternate rows, which together produced a mixed rhythm — one column here, two there — and, where a lone checkpoint sat in a row of its own, a zebra band that stopped halfway across the panel. Reported as exactly that. It is now **one column top to bottom**: every checkpoint is its own row, a band always spans the full width, and there is no pairing left to reason about — the row index simply *is* the visual row, so the placement computation that mirrored CSS auto-placement is gone with it. Each checkpoint number is now a **step marker**: 24px, monospaced, tabular figures so the column of numerals lines up, and quiet in colour so it marks the step without competing with the checkpoint's name. That is worth the pixels here because this is a numbered standard — the numeral is how a reader crossreferences a finding against PAC or the Matterhorn document itself. The panel is taller than before, and that is the trade: 31 rows read top to bottom rather than tiled, which is what makes each step visible on a document with many findings. **No new attack surface**: CSS classes and one `data-testid`; no route, parameter, dependency, stored field, or retention change, and the honesty contract (no aggregate count, no bare "Pass") is untouched and still pinned. Tests 2,896 → 2,897.



### v1.114.0 — 2026-08-28 · The Matterhorn checklist is zebra striped by visual row (layout only)

Follow-on to v1.113.0, which fixed *which* findings belong to which checkpoint; this makes the boundary between checkpoints visible rather than something the reader traces. The subtlety is that a row here is a **visual** row, not a list item: a checkpoint with findings occupies a whole row alone while two clean ones share one, so `nth-child` striping would band one half of a pair and leave the other bare — a rendering fault, not a stripe. The band follows the row the grid actually places each checkpoint in (computed the same way CSS auto-placement does, including a full-width item meeting a half-filled row and starting the next), and both halves of a shared row always match. Grid gaps became cell padding so the bands meet edge to edge, with a matching negative margin on the list so a band bleeds to the card's inner edge instead of eating cell width — without it the longer checkpoint names wrapped their status chip onto a second line. The band is `--surface-raised` (#16191f on #111111 dark, #eef2f7 on #ffffff light); `--surface-card-alt` was tried first and is three levels of grey — correct in principle, invisible in practice, which fails the stripe's only job, and it was rejected on the rendered page rather than in review. **No new attack surface**: CSS classes and one `data-visual-row` attribute; no route, parameter, dependency, stored field, or retention change, and the panel's honesty contract (no aggregate count, no bare "Pass") is untouched and still pinned. Tests 2,895 → 2,896.



### v1.113.0 — 2026-08-28 · The Matterhorn checklist reads properly on a document with many findings (layout only)

Reported from a real report: _"for files with lots of errors, it can be difficult to see what goes with what."_ The 31 checkpoints tiled two-up in a grid, and a grid row is as tall as its tallest cell — so checkpoint 01 with two long veraPDF clauses sat beside an empty 02, and 06 with three clauses sat beside a one-line 05, producing a column of dead space and leaving the reader to work out which heading a block of findings belonged to. A checkpoint **with** findings now takes the full row, so its evidence can only sit beneath its own heading; the one-line clean checkpoints keep tiling two-up. The evidence list also gained a rail tying it to the checkpoint above it, and each line is a two-column grid with a **fixed-width** tag column — each line being its own grid, an auto column sized to its own row and every description started at a different x, including the plain-language findings that carry no `veraPDF` marker. A wrapped ISO clause now hangs under its own text instead of running back to the margin. **Nothing about what the panel says changed**: same checkpoints, same statuses, same evidence, and the honesty contract (no aggregate count, no bare "Pass") is untouched and still pinned. **No new attack surface**: CSS classes and one `data-testid` per row; no route, parameter, dependency, stored field, or retention change. Verified by eye as well as by test — rendered against a 246-page report carrying the exact clauses from the report that prompted this, and screenshotted at full-panel and single-checkpoint zoom. Tests 2,893 → 2,895.



### v1.112.0 — 2026-08-28 · Byte counters on `/status` gain a human-readable twin (payload addition; no new surface)

The JSON published `free_bytes: 58131922944`, `total_bytes: 82086711296` and `size_bytes: 89382608` and left the reader to do the arithmetic the HTML page had been doing for years ("54.1 GB free of 76.4 GB"). Each counter now carries the formatted figure beside it — `free_human`, `total_human`, `size_human` — the same pairing the payload already used for time (`finished_at` / `finished_at_chicago`); the raw counters are unchanged and remain authoritative. **One formatter now serves both surfaces**: the page carried its own copy, the payload would have needed a second, and two implementations of one format is precisely how a page ends up reading "54.1 GB" beside a payload reading "54.2 GB". It moved to `@file-audit/shared`, the page's duplicate is gone, and the page's test asserts against the shared helper rather than a literal, so reintroducing a local formatter fails the moment its output differs. One behaviour change fell out of it: a size that cannot be read now renders as **"unknown" rather than "0 B"** — an unreadable figure must not look like an empty disk, the same reasoning that makes `null` the payload's answer for an unqueryable filesystem. **No new attack surface**: two derived strings computed from numbers already published, no route, parameter, dependency, stored field, or retention change. `statusPrivacy.test.ts` pins the exact key set of `payload.disk`, so the addition is explicit and reviewed rather than silent. Tests 2,884 → 2,893.



### v1.111.0 — 2026-08-28 · Detached tag subtrees no longer count against a document (false-positive fix; a C becomes an A)

**A second dispute in two days, and the remediator was right again.** The report on `coltons-task-force-2025-A0.pdf` flagged two images as lacking alternative text; the remediator answered that "the two figures aren't in the text". The file settles it: the live tag tree holds **four `<Figure>` elements and all four carry `/Alt`**. The two the report named sit in a **detached `<Part>`** — no `/P`, referenced by nothing — alongside a leftover paragraph and two headings for a page that was rebuilt. The subtree hangs off nothing, is in no ParentTree, and carries no `/Pg`: on no page, reachable by no screen reader. **79/C → 100/A.** Root cause was our reachability test, not the document: orphan pruning (added for InDesign leftovers) asked of each element *alone* whether it carried a `/P` or was named by some `/K`, and both hold for an element inside a subtree that is itself an orphan — the figures point at their orphaned parent and the parent names them. Reachability is a property of the path to the root, so it is now traced from the root: `collectLiveStructRefs` walks `/K` transitively (iterative, visited-guarded, bounded at 500k nodes), and the old test survives only as a fallback for a root whose `/K` cannot be resolved, so a parse quirk can never prune a whole document. The gate covers figures, tables, lists, formulas and notes; headings were already safe, being re-collected by a live tree walk. **Swept all 29 controls: not one score or grade moved** — the change is visible only on documents carrying leftovers. The file was produced by **Canva**, and the fragment is the ordinary residue of a page rebuilt after tagging; nothing about the submitted document needed fixing. **No new attack surface**: one reachability walk over data already parsed, no route, parameter, dependency, stored field, or retention change. Verified against the object graph before any code changed — 10 objects carry `/S /Figure`, 4 live, 4 already correctly pruned, exactly 2 in the detached subtree. Tests 2,882 → 2,884.



### v1.110.0 — 2026-08-28 · Headings are scored on what they say, not only on their levels (scoring change, corpus-swept)

**Prompted by a request to verify a grade rather than trust it**, and every claim below was checked against the PDF's own structures — tag tree, RoleMap, page content streams — before it was acted on. The 246-page report from v1.109.0 scored 60/Moderate for headings on the strength of six level skips. Its outline was far worse than that: of 96 heading tags, **19 carry no text at all, 14 hold an entire paragraph, and 29 are cut mid-word** (_"Population d"_, _"property crime a"_, _"la"_). The report was already printing those fragments and scoring none of them. Heading structure now scores that: an outline where a fifth of the tags are empty, fragmentary, or paragraph-length costs the same as a broken hierarchy, and half or more costs the same as having no levels at all. That document lands at 40/Moderate for headings, 53/F overall (from 56/F). **Corpus-swept: 26 of the 27 controls come out identical**; the only mover is the document that prompted the review. A second fix retires a finding that misdirected: _"13 list(s) have items missing `<LBody>`"_ on a file that contains 43 list bodies spelled `/Lbody` — a single capital off the standard name, unmapped in the RoleMap — so the advice was to add content that was already present. Near-miss tag names are now identified as what they are, with the one-line RoleMap repair, for any standard type. **The reason this release is worth reading twice**: the first cut of the heading check dropped a conformance-clean control (`DVFR_Biennial_Report_2024`) from **100/A to 79/C**, and investigating rather than accepting it exposed a genuine extraction limit — on some pages pdf.js emits marked-content boundaries as immediately-closed empty pairs and delivers the text separately (168 text items, 17 ids, text for one), so five ordinary `<H1>` tags read as empty. Those pages are now excluded from the census rather than counted against the document. **No new attack surface**: no route, parameter, dependency, stored field, or retention change — one extraction census, one scoring rule, and copy. The new rule is deliberately reluctant (six headings minimum, three bad minimum, a fifth of the outline affected, ordinary English explicitly protected) because any sub-100 category becomes a severity and a severity caps the grade. Tests 2,865 → 2,882.



### v1.109.1 — 2026-08-28 · The proxy was still enforcing the old one-minute limit (deploy contract; no new attack surface)

**Found by verifying the previous release against production rather than assuming it.** v1.109.0 raised the audit's own budget to two minutes; nginx's `location /api/` block still carried `proxy_read_timeout 60s`. The same 246-page report returned an nginx HTML `504 Gateway Time-out` at **61.9s** — invisible from inside the process: no route error, no log line, no failed-audit row, because nginx hung up on a request the app was still legitimately working on. Exactly the 2026-08-13 `client_max_body_size` shape, and the second time a limit *in front of* the app has broken a working feature with a green suite. With `proxy_read_timeout 180s` the same file returns **HTTP 200 in 59.8s** with both veraPDF verdicts attached — it was finishing within a second of the old ceiling, which is why it failed at all. **The browser's audit page was never affected** (it creates a job and polls, so every request is short); the exposed callers were the synchronous `/api/analyze` fallback and `/api/audit-url`, **which the fleet scanner uses** on documents of this size. Recorded as `DEPLOY.NGINX_PROXY_READ_TIMEOUT_S` (180) + `PROXY_TIMEOUT_HEADROOM_S` (30) with five `deployLimits.test.ts` assertions, since the value lives in Forge and nothing in the repository can enforce it. One of those tests exists because v1.109.0 moved the arithmetic: qpdf and pdfjs no longer overlap, so their budgets **add** (30s + 120s = 150s) rather than max — restoring the `Promise.all` would silently make this ceiling over-generous, and now fails the build instead. **No new attack surface**: no route, parameter, dependency, stored field, or retention change; one proxy timeout, two documented constants, and their guard tests. The proxy still gives up eventually — 180s is pinned below a 300s ceiling, and every step inside the app remains individually bounded. Tests 2,860 → 2,865.



### v1.109.0 — 2026-08-28 · A document reported as "too complex" was not — the audit was starving itself (resource contention; no new attack surface)

**A user-reported failure, reproduced end to end on production and traced to this tool's own scheduling.** A 246-page InDesign annual report failed with `504 This file is too complex to analyze within the time limit`. It is not complex: on the production droplet qpdf parses it in **1.7s** (3.1 MB of JSON, 33 MB RSS) and pdfjs in **14.2s**. The server log named the thrower — `Error: QPDF timeout … killed: true` — and qpdf was not doing 30 seconds of work. `analyzePDF` ran both passes under one `Promise.all`, but **pdfjs runs in-process**, so Node only drained qpdf's stdout pipe between pdfjs page chunks; qpdf blocked on a full pipe while its own subprocess timeout ran, making `QPDF_TIMEOUT_MS` measure the whole audit's wall clock. Compounding it, the two veraPDF passes ran simultaneously at **785 MB RSS and 191% CPU each** on a 2-vCPU / 3.9 GB box already 583 MB into swap, stretching pdfjs to 41s. Measured progression: qpdf alone **1.7s** → concurrent with pdfjs **15.7s** → with both JVMs **killed at 30s**, matching the 39.2s production 504 exactly. Fixes: qpdf now completes **before** pdfjs starts (the overlap was never real — 15.7s concurrent vs 1.7 + 14.2 sequential — and the qpdf phase now measures **2.7s under full contention**); `VERAPDF_MAX_CONCURRENT: 2 → 1` serializes the passes and halves peak memory; `PDFJS_TIMEOUT_MS` 60s → 120s with veraPDF's per-pass budget 30s → 45s (one PDF/UA pass measured **25.3s idle** on this file, so 30s was silently losing the verdict) and its queue budget 60s → 90s so the second pass can wait out the first. **The "audit server offline" and "degraded" badges were the same event, not an outage:** the health endpoint could not get CPU either, and a failed engine probe was cached for the full 10 minutes — /status reported veraPDF down while it answered `--version` in 2.4s. Transient probe failures now expire after 60s (`STATUS.ENGINE_PROBE_FAILURE_TTL_MS`); `not_configured`/`not_executable` keep the long TTL so a genuinely absent engine is not re-probed every minute. **Reviewed for surface: none is added** — no new route, parameter, dependency, stored field, or retention change; the diff is scheduling, two timeout constants, one cache TTL, and copy. The user-facing message stopped diagnosing the document (it was reached from a catch-all on `err.killed` — any killed subprocess) and now says the cause is usually timing, asks for a retry first, and suggests splitting the document last; one shared `AUDIT_TIMEOUT_MESSAGE` serves all six surfaces that can produce it. One latent test defect surfaced and was fixed: the fleet route's per-entry error mapping was hand-copied into its own test and had drifted to assert that an unmatched error echoes `err.message` — which the route never does, an earlier P2 info-leak finding; the mapping is now exported and the test exercises production. Only **17 timeout failures exist in the entire audit log**, three of them this document during diagnosis, so nothing needs re-running. Tests 2,843 → 2,860.



### v1.108.0 — 2026-08-27 · Table headers get told which way they point, and the PDF/UA vs WCAG split is explained (copy only; no scoring change)

A second document from the same reviewer, and this time **the app's diagnosis was right** — verified against the file rather than taken on trust: not one of its 16 table cells carries an `/A` attribute dictionary, so no `/Scope`, no `/Headers`, no IDs, on a genuine two-way table with labels across the top *and* down the left. What was wrong was the advice. The fix text said _set Scope to "Column" or "Row"_ without saying which goes where, and the Visual view's step said _"mark a header row"_ to an author whose header row was already marked. Both now state the rule by position — top cells are Column, left-hand cells are Row, the corner cell needs nothing — plus the Word shortcut that writes the scopes automatically. Reviewed as **copy only**: no detector, threshold, route, or stored field changed, the document still scores 89/B, and the 185-test calibration passes untouched. The substantive addition is the **standards distinction**, because authors keep colliding with it: PDF/UA treats a header with no direction as a defect outright, while WCAG only asks that the relationship be determinable somehow and never names Scope as the only way — so an author can honestly say a file passes WCAG while this report still asks for Scope, and **both are being truthful**. The copy now says that out loud, which is what the scoring was already doing quietly: the conformance verdict for this file remains "no automated failures" with zero confirmed WCAG failures, so this is reported as a PDF/UA readiness gap and never as a WCAG violation. Tests 2,838 → 2,843.



### v1.107.0 — 2026-08-27 · Reading order is no longer scored on forms; neutral findings stop wearing a failure mark (scoring change, deliberately re-baselined)

**A user-reported false positive, confirmed and fixed.** A DoIT Accessibility XFA example scored 65/D for reading order and its own author disputed the grade — correctly. The entire deduction traced to four `/Caption` elements reading _"Order Date:", "City:", "State:", "ZIP:"_: tagged exactly where a reader meets them, painted last, because that is how form content renders. In a form the two orders are *expected* to disagree, so the better the tagging the worse the metric scored. The card had also been printing _"divergence is not automatically wrong"_ directly above a 35-point deduction for that divergence. Reading order is now reported-but-unscored on documents with form fields, and that file scores 100/A. **This is a scoring change and it is treated as one**: the 185-test calibration passes unchanged, a re-sweep of all 27 control PDFs shows every non-form document keeping its exact score, and a test pins that a *perfectly* ordered form is unscored too — so the rule is structural, not an amnesty for forms that would score badly. Reviewed for what it could hide: an unassessed category counts as passing, so a genuinely bad form no longer loses points here; that is accepted deliberately, because the measurement could not tell the two apart and a wrong verdict is worse than an honest "not measurable — check it this way". Two smaller corrections ship alongside: the report card was choosing one icon from the *category* score and stamping it on every line, marking plain measurements and its own caveat as failures (now classified per line, with the load-bearing test being the negative one — a real fault must never be softened into a bullet); and the lettering note fired its full correction on a banner that already had alt text, under a category scoring 100/A, so it now credits the author and marks itself advisory. Tests 2,827 → 2,838.

### v1.106.0 — 2026-08-27 · The lettering finding reaches the Visual view, and its explanation is corrected (copy + presentation; no new attack surface)

Two things, one of them a correction to the release below. **The warning now appears in the Visual view**, where most authors actually read their report, as an action-plan step that answers *"what images? I never added one"* before asking for anything. And **v1.105.0's explanation was wrong**: it asserted that Word had flattened effect-carrying text into a picture, so the remedy was to remove the effect and re-export. Re-reading the reference agenda's content stream showed its letterhead words were **vector outlines** — 194 bezier curves, zero text operators — lettering inside placed artwork, which no export setting can recover. An author following that advice would have searched Word for an effect that never existed. The finding is renamed and now names both causes with their different remedies, only one of which is repairable. Also corrected: v1.105.0 claimed every hand-checked flag was a true positive; inspecting the remaining two found **three true and one false** (a chart's solid callout-box background, whose text extracts fine). Reviewed as **copy and presentation only** — no new file access, route, schema, or stored field; the detector, its thresholds, and the scoring calibration are untouched, so all 27 control documents keep byte-identical scores. One behavioural change worth recording: a per-document Acrobat block normally outranks dictionary copy, and here it was opening with *"Add alternate text"* — the exact instruction the step exists to prevent — so a variant lead-in now precedes it while keeping the block's valid advice for real photos. That was caught by rendering a real document, not by a unit test. The v1.105.0 announcement banner was corrected **in place** rather than re-shown, since that release was tagged but never deployed and no visitor read the original wording (v1.73.0 precedent); this entry is the disclosure. Tests 2,818 → 2,827.

### v1.105.0 — 2026-08-27 · A new finding: text turned into pictures (read-only analysis; no new attack surface, nothing new collected)

A real board agenda exposed a defect this tool could see and was not reporting: its letterhead — the agency's own name — had been turned into pictures by Word's PDF export, one image per line, so "ILLINOIS" appeared nowhere in the text layer. It could not be read aloud, searched, or reflowed, and the report said only "3 images missing alt text", sending the author to Acrobat to describe their own letterhead when the fix belonged in Word. Reviewed as **read-only analysis of bytes the analyzer already parsed**: the detector reads `/Width` and `/Height` from the qpdf object graph the parser had already walked — no new file access, no new process, no new route, no schema change, and nothing new stored or transmitted. It emits **findings text only** and is barred from the arithmetic: the 185-test scoring calibration passes unchanged, so all 27 control documents keep byte-identical scores and grades. The honesty discipline is the load-bearing part and is enforced by test: the heuristic recognises a *shape*, which is evidence rather than proof, so the finding asks the reader to confirm it themselves and never asserts a WCAG failure. False positives were hunted rather than assumed — a control file's state seal, flattened into six identical 392-pixel bands, matched the shape test on all six until a width-grouping rule dropped it (7 flags → 1), and decorative colour bars (1274×194, 2390×199) forced the height ceiling down from 200 to 120. Across the control corpus it fires on four of 27 files, and the ones inspected by hand are true positives, including a fact sheet whose section title was a picture. Copy is written for a non-technical reader by request — no "rasterized", no "artifact". Tests 2,805 → 2,818.

### v1.104.0 — 2026-08-27 · The acknowledgment notice is withdrawn before it ever shipped (removal only; attack surface shrinks)

The notice added in v1.102.0 and hardened in v1.103.0 is removed at the maintainer's request, pending a rethink of how it should look and work. Reviewed as a **pure removal**: the dialog, the page freeze, the drop-zone and remediation gates, the shared state, the stored `localStorage` timestamp, and the `AUTOMATION_ACK_HOURS` setting are all gone, along with the one piece of client-side state this application had ever written for its own purposes. Nothing was added in their place. Uploading, checking, and remediating behave exactly as they did in v1.101.0. The material fact for this record: **v1.102.0 and v1.103.0 were tagged but never deployed**, so no visitor ever saw the notice, no browser ever stored an acknowledgment, and there is nothing to migrate or clean up — the two entries below describe code that existed only on `main`. The **disclosure itself is unaffected**: every report still carries the automation-limit band at every grade, with the sourced coverage figures and the line that a good score does not mean the document is accessible; the landing-page announcement now describes that band instead of the withdrawn notice. Tests 2,831 → 2,805, the 26 removed being the notice's own.

### v1.103.0 — 2026-08-26 · The gate freezes the page and becomes a real dialog; the wording is rewritten in plain language (presentation only; no new attack surface)

Two user requests, one release. **The page is now frozen until the acknowledgment is given** — it does not scroll, and a dimmed backdrop shows why. Reviewed as a **presentation change with no server component**: enforcement, the one-week window, the fail-closed behaviour, and the privacy posture are byte-for-byte the v1.102.0 logic; no request, route, header, column, retention window, or score changed, and the acknowledgment is still one `localStorage` timestamp that never leaves the browser. The accessibility reasoning is the load-bearing part, so it is recorded rather than assumed: this **reverses v1.102.0's "deliberately not a modal"**, and the reversal is the *accessible* choice. A scroll lock without dialog semantics is the genuinely broken option — keyboard users would still tab into content they can no longer scroll to, screen-reader users would get no signal that the page behind is inert — so freezing brings `role="dialog"`, `aria-modal="true"`, a label and description, and contained focus with it. No Escape handler, deliberately: the acknowledgment is required, the button is the only way out, and focus starts there. A dialog released by its own control is not a WCAG 2.1.2 keyboard trap. Two failure modes are pinned by test because they would be severe and silent: the freeze is lifted on **unmount** as well as on acknowledgment, so a frozen page can never outlive the gate, and it is never applied at all to someone who already acknowledged. **The wording is now written for a non-technical reader** and a jargon guard fails the suite if "alt text", "reading order", "WCAG", "PDF/UA", or a tool acronym returns to that copy. Tests 2,825 → 2,831.

### v1.102.0 — 2026-08-26 · The automation-coverage disclosure becomes a gate: no file is checked until it is acknowledged (client-side only; no new attack surface, nothing new collected)

Legal compliance: people must understand this tool checks only *part* of accessibility — roughly 30–40% of issues in independent testing, true of every checker — before they use it. Reviewed as a **client-side control with no server component**: the acknowledgment is one `localStorage` timestamp, never a cookie, never transmitted, and no identity is recorded, so no request, route, header, column, or retention window changed. State the limit of that honestly — it evidences the disclosure was made and required on a device, not who agreed to it; recording *who* would mean identifying visitors, which this service deliberately does not do (§ 7). It **fails closed**: absent, junk, future-dated, or unreadable storage all leave the tool gated, so a tampered value cannot open it. Enforcement is defense-in-depth rather than a single check — the drop zone refuses the picker, drops, and staged submits; the page guards `analyzeFile`/`analyzeBatch` independently; `RemediateButton` refuses at both entry points — and it is deliberately **not a modal** (no backdrop, no focus trap, no `aria-modal`; the page stays scrollable and readable), because a focus trap would introduce an accessibility defect into an accessibility tool. Verified in a browser against the production build, not only in tests: with the acknowledgment cleared, a click opened no file picker and a genuine `drop` event carrying a PDF started no audit; the ack persisted across reload, and a backdated or junk value re-gated the tool. Also in this release: the automation-limit band is ungated on every grade and every report, and its coverage percentages are sourced and linked (UK GDS, Deque, Adobe, Matterhorn) — amending, not repealing, the v1.58.1 rule against bare figures near the grade, enforced as a test whitelist. Tests 2,799 → 2,825.

### v1.101.0 — 2026-08-26 · The Matterhorn checklist states its scope: PDFs only, Office formats still fully checked (copy + pins; no new attack surface)

A scope note at the top of the landing-page Matterhorn checklist answers "why is this checklist only about PDFs?" — the protocol is PDF/UA's test model and applies to one format by construction, while Word/PowerPoint/Excel files are still fully checked here under their own per-format audits. Reviewed for what the change could expose: nothing — static repository-authored copy rendered through ordinary interpolation; the one new outbound link is `target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer"` to the PDF Association's own Matterhorn Protocol page (the same URL the section already linked); the internal link routes to the existing technical-details page. Both halves of the claim are test-pinned so neither wrong reading — Office files aren't checked, or Matterhorn applies to them — can creep back. No API, scoring, or storage change. Tests 2,796 → 2,799.

### v1.100.0 — 2026-08-26 · Real per-pass progress: the job-model audit (new endpoints, reviewed; privacy posture unchanged and stated)

Two additive endpoints give the page real observed step states — the engine analysis and the two veraPDF passes each flip pending/running/done as the pipeline reports them, never a percentage (the JVM passes expose none; a percent would be invented, and the pin says so). Reviewed as new surface: the pipeline itself is the SAME code (extracted verbatim into a shared core; the 54 pre-existing route tests pass unchanged against the thinned synchronous handler — the extraction is proven faithful, not assumed); the job store is an in-memory Map only — no disk, no database, no identity, an unguessable once-returned token stored as its SHA-256 and compared timing-safe, with wrong-id and wrong-token indistinguishable (no existence oracle); results delivered exactly once and deleted, 10-minute TTL, 100-job cap, 5-minute hard-timeout backstop. The finished report's brief wait in process memory is DESCRIBED in the policy (v1.17, § 2/§ 14) rather than assumed unchanged. The synchronous endpoint is untouched and remains the page's automatic fallback, so a deploy skew can never break uploads. Tests 2,783 → 2,796.

### v1.99.1 — 2026-08-26 · The veraPDF queue lines enumerated honestly (copy + pin only)

The waiting screen's two veraPDF lines now read "pass 1 of 2 / pass 2 of 2, both run together." The qualifier is the point: the passes run concurrently and the server reports nothing until both finish, so a bare "pass 2 of 2" would claim sequential progress the page cannot know. The test pin carries the same rationale. Nothing received, sent, stored, or scored differently. Tests unchanged: 2,783.

### v1.99.0 — 2026-08-26 · The waiting experience: a rotating check queue, elapsed counter, and up-front timing expectation (client-side presentation only)

Prompted by a real report of an audit that "just spins": a design-heavy InDesign PDF measured ~26 s in production (the two veraPDF JVM passes are the slow part) while the overlay showed one frozen — and for Office files, wrong — stage line. The overlay now cycles through the real per-format check suite with an elapsed counter and escalating truthful reassurance (15 s: large PDFs can take 30–60 seconds; 60 s: every step is hard-timeout-bounded, so it finishes or fails cleanly). Reviewed for honesty rather than attack surface, since nothing new is received, sent, or stored: the server reports nothing until done, so the queue cycles rather than claiming step-by-step progress, never fakes completion, and URL audits keep their genuinely-known milestones; the rotation is visual-only for screen readers, which get a ~15-second live-region cadence instead of a 2.5-second firehose. The drop zone states up front that analysis can take up to a minute. Tests 2,777 → 2,783.

### v1.98.0 — 2026-08-26 · The Technical Details unification: one content source for the page and the collapsible (no new attack surface)

The `/technical-details` page and the audit page's Technical Details collapsible are now the same `TechnicalExplainer` component — identical by construction and pinned by test (page chrome only on the page; no section prose allowed back; the header states the identity claim so any future divergence would make the page lie visibly). The motivation is the v1.95.2 lesson: two separately-authored surfaces drift, and one of them taught a scoring model retired six weeks earlier. Nothing unique was lost — the worked scoring example (current model + severity cap), the WCAG 2.2 Alignment section, and the toolchain license table moved into the shared component, so both surfaces gained them. Reviewed for surface: purely a re-composition of already-served, repo-authored content — no new route, no request, no input handling, nothing collected or stored; the data-retention policy is untouched at v1.16. Tests 2,775 → 2,777.

### v1.97.0 — 2026-08-26 · The WCAG second opinion: veraPDF's machine-testable WCAG 2.2 profile on every PDF audit (new subprocess input reviewed; adversarially tested)

The deferred Matterhorn-audit item, built scoping-first: a read-only server probe established that veraPDF 1.30.1 has no WCAG flavour and ships no profile files, so the machine-testable WCAG 2.2 profile is vendored in-repo (byte-identical to upstream `rel/1.30` @ `bc8e773`, provenance documented, verified against the production engine before integration) and runs as a second concurrent JVM per PDF audit via `--profile`. Security posture: the pass reuses the existing secret-stripped, timeout-bounded, output-capped invocation path; the profile file is repository-static (not derived from any upload); ONE shared temp copy serves both passes with the first concurrency slot still gating the write — the pre-existing "queued uploads never spill to disk" hardening pin caught the first draft breaking that invariant, and the design was corrected rather than the test. The new panel renders word statuses only (never "Pass"/conformance — it says so itself), shows the v1.91.0 "Did not run" disclosure when the check could not run, renders nothing for the absent key (pre-v1.97.0 stored reports; the `VERAPDF_WCAG_ENABLED=false` kill switch), and bounds forged shared-report payloads to a top-20 render with visible truncation — attack-replayed with a 5,000-row flood. A failing second opinion is test-pinned to change nothing about the score, grade, or categories; calibration is byte-identical across all 32 controls. Data-retention policy v1.16 (lifecycle unchanged — stated, not assumed). Tests 2,752 → 2,775. Both Matterhorn surfaces also gained the plain-language law linkage (ADA Title II + IITAA → WCAG → Matterhorn), with the "the law requires WCAG, not a PDF/UA badge" precision line test-pinned on both.

### v1.96.0 — 2026-08-26 · The data-retention policy verified and reshaped (presentation and wording; policy v1.15; no data-practice change)

The policy page got the full-read verification the other explanation surfaces received, plus three user-requested usability changes. Content verdict: accurate — §§ 1–3, 5–9, 11–15 checked out (the PDF/UA fixes had shipped in v1.95.1); § 13's glossary gained a PDF/UA-2 note. The reshaping: a "Last updated" date leads the header, test-pinned to § 14's newest entry so it cannot drift; § 4's AI exclusion list now names providers and model families without version numbers (the rationale stated in the policy itself — versions churn too fast to chase, and the retired "Bard"/"PaLM" names were the proof), covering every past and future version; § 7's 13-row retention wall became three color-banded tables (document / application records / adjacent systems) with scannable retention chips and every fact string verbatim — the overclaim-guard and row-content pins all pass unchanged; § 10 collapses 148 earlier reviews into one native details fold with the newest expanded, every entry still in the DOM and still counted by its test. Reviewed as change surface: no new route, no request, nothing new collected or stored, no retention period moved — the § 10 card markup moved into a child component that receives only compiled repo data, keeping the v-html safety argument intact and test-pinned. Tests 2,751 → 2,752.

### v1.95.2 — 2026-08-26 · The standalone technical-details page corrected (copy only; the second, separately-authored explanation surface)

The `/technical-details` manager tour is a separate surface from the audit page's Technical Details expander v1.95.1 fixed, and reading it in full found twelve issues — two long-standing. Worst: its worked scoring example still taught the pre-v1.58.3 weight-redistribution model (not-applicable categories count as passing and keep their weight since v1.58.3 — the example's own answer was wrong under the current model, and it never mentioned the severity cap; it now shows both mechanisms, ending at the capped 79 · C). Also corrected: the OOXML path described as "no subprocess" while the diagram below it showed the child process; Office Form Accessibility called "omitted" when the card renders as Not assessed (now with the v1.95.0 censuses named); and four PDF/UA-1-only labels gained the PDF/UA-2 clause (the remediation stage correctly keeps ua1). Copy only — nothing new collected, no new route, no scoring change; data-retention policy stays v1.14. Tests unchanged: 2,751.

### v1.95.1 — 2026-08-26 · The accuracy pass: every explanatory surface verified against the v1.91–v1.95 arc (copy only; no code behavior change)

A user-requested verification of the front-page teasers, the Technical Details page (read in full, all 2,265 lines), the data-retention policy, the README, and both llms docs against everything the five-release Matterhorn arc changed. Fourteen fixes on Technical Details alone: the extraction tables gained the v1.92–v1.94 census rows, four category cards now describe the current scoring rungs, and two pre-existing errors surfaced — the Bookmarks/Reading Order cards showed swapped weights (the weight table beside them was right, so the page contradicted itself), and the Regression Guards section described a three-profile comparison the code has not performed since the dual-profile system was retired in v1.21.0 (it checks two: overall + strict). Three data-retention sections and the README/llms docs no longer describe veraPDF as PDF/UA-1-only or remediation-only (it validates PDF/UA-2 when declared, on every PDF audit). Retention facts, endpoints, and payload shapes verified unchanged; the JSON export's `"PDF/UA (ISO 14289-1)"` literal is deliberately untouched as a documented payload contract. Nothing new collected, no new route, no scoring change — data-retention policy stays v1.14. Tests unchanged: 2,751.

### v1.95.0 — 2026-08-26 · The Office pass: theme colors resolved for contrast, censuses disclosed (new document-internal readers, adversarially reviewed)

Extends the Matterhorn audit's remaining backlog to Word and Excel. The headline: colors referencing the document theme — how most Office text is actually colored — now resolve through the theme part and feed the contrast check (Word `themeColor`/`themeTint`/`themeShade`/`themeFill`; Excel `theme=` with tint in Excel's light-first index order, plus the legacy 66-entry `indexed=` palette with workbook overrides), where the checker previously stayed silent on anything not written out explicitly; a failing theme pair asserts a confirmed WCAG 1.4.3. New disclosed censuses: Word run-level languages (evidence-based 3.1.2), floating/anchored objects (1.3.2), merged and empty table rows, blank-paragraph spacing, content controls and legacy form fields; Excel hidden-sheet exclusion, far-from-A1 data starts, and legacy form/OLE controls — the form cards on both formats are now evidence-based instead of an unconditional "uncommon" claim. Every new reader of document internals was reviewed as attack surface, each attack replayed by a test: forged kilobyte `w:lang` values are BCP 47-validated before they can reach a report string, a forged million-entry `indexedColors` override caps at 256 with past-cap indices honestly unresolved, and out-of-range theme indices or junk tints resolve to *unresolved*, never a defaulted color. An independent high-effort review then confirmed ten more findings, all fixed before tagging — the worst being every `w:sdt` counted as a form control (an automatic Table of Contents got a false "content control detected" claim on one of the most common document shapes), `Number("") === 0` resolving a malformed empty `theme` attribute to white (able to fabricate a confirmed white-on-white 1.4.3 from a corrupt attribute), and split-`instrText` legacy fields reading as "No form controls were detected" — alongside table-cell paragraphs chaining into false spacing runs, baseline-less language censuses contradicting their own report, image-only rows called empty, body-only evidence phrased as whole-document claims, unreadable palette overrides silently swapped for the colors they overrode, and two web surfaces still carrying pre-v1.95.0 copy. Calibration: two real-world Excel controls flip Color Contrast from N/A to a checked, passing 100; no score, grade, or verdict moved anywhere — including through all ten review fixes; the 27 control PDFs byte-identical a fifth consecutive release. No new route, no schema change, nothing new collected or stored — data-retention policy stays v1.14. Tests 2,723 → 2,751.

### v1.94.0 — 2026-08-26 · The score-integrity pass plus the requested adversarial red/blue — thirteen findings fixed before tagging (new analyzer censuses; hardening; no new attack surface)

The final tranche of the 2026-08-25 Matterhorn audit's PDF backlog, shipped only after an adversarial review of the whole v1.91.0 → v1.94.0 surface. New machine checks: extracted characters with no usable text mapping (PUA/U+FFFD — Matterhorn 10) and visible text painted outside every tagged run (01-005/006), both measured from the text-layer item stream so annotation appearance streams cannot pollute them and threshold-tiered so symbol-font bullets stay advisory; visible form-field widgets no structure element references via OBJR reduce the form score proportionally and assert a confirmed 1.3.1; reference-XObject (30-001), embedded-attachment /Desc (21, /EF required), and signature-presence (23) censuses; PDF/UA-2 flavour detection for veraPDF. The red/blue pass fixed THIRTEEN findings before this tag: my own sweep's RB-1 (hostile single-chain RoleMap made resolution quadratic in the main process — hop- and entry-capped, proven by a 10,000-entry-chain wall-clock test), RB-2 (forged shared-report verdicts flooding the Matterhorn panel with DOM nodes — bounded at every dimension), RB-3 (megabyte /Note IDs held whole — bounded prefix); and an independent high-effort review's ten confirmed findings, the worst being F1 — OBJRs in exotic-but-legal serializations were invisible, so a correctly tagged form read as all-untagged and earned a FALSE confirmed Level A failure — alongside a same-page anchor that never opened, a gate firing beside an N/A category, advisory census lines flipping panel rows and action-plan headlines, /EF-less external file references counted as attachments, pre-census stored reports rendering green for checks that never existed, silently-truncated unmapped lists contradicting the panel's own promise, press-production annotations failing press-ready PDFs, and a collapse that had cost the landing page its heading/landmark semantics. Every fix is test-pinned; nothing renders unescaped; no new route, no schema change beyond an optional census-generation field on results, nothing new collected or stored — data-retention policy stays v1.14. Calibration: all 27 control PDFs byte-identical, fourth release running. Tests 2,671 → 2,723.

### v1.93.0 — 2026-08-26 · Every PDF report gains a per-document Matterhorn view (presentation-only regrouping; no new attack surface)

The report pages now carry a collapsed "Your document against the Matterhorn checklist" panel on all four surfaces, regrouping the findings the report already shows under the 31 checkpoints the landing page discloses. Reviewed for what the change could expose: nothing — the projection is computed in the browser from the same report payload the page already renders; no new route, parameter, request, or stored field, and every rendered string is either repo-authored copy, an already-rendered finding fragment, or a veraPDF clause/description that the PDF/UA panel already displays through the same escaped interpolation. The honesty contract is pinned by test: no aggregate count or percentage (a "24 of 31" beside a grade reads as a second grade), never the word "Pass", human-judgment checkpoints can never claim machine coverage at report level, veraPDF-covered checkpoints render "Not machine-checked" whenever veraPDF did not run (the v1.91.0 tri-state carried down), and unmappable veraPDF rules land in a visible bucket rather than being dropped. A real captured analyzer payload guards the finding-marker mapping against silent drift. URL page-audit rows (no categories) and non-PDF reports never render the panel. Tests 2,650 → 2,671.


### v1.92.0 — 2026-08-26 · The small Matterhorn completeness pass — eight new machine checks, zero score drift on the control corpus (new analyzer censuses; no new attack surface)

The second tranche of the 2026-08-25 Matterhorn completeness audit. New in-house checks: `<Note>` /ID presence and uniqueness (Matterhorn 19 — advisory), `<Formula>` text alternatives (17 — scored into alt_text and asserted as a confirmed 1.1.1 failure when absent, the same bar figures are held to), mixed generic-`<H>`-plus-numbered-headings (14-002 — scored 60, a real PDF/UA prohibition the heading check previously tolerated), `/Lang` value shape ("english"/"en_US" earn half credit with a targeted fix, never a gate assertion), RoleMap validity (02 — circular chains, remapped standard types, unmapped custom tags, all advisory) with **transitive RoleMap resolution** (a `Custom → MyPara → P` chain previously resolved one hop, silently dropping chained headings/tables/figures from every census), JavaScript and multimedia presence censuses (29/05 — disclosures plus a 1.2.2 not-assessed entry when media exists; three Adobe-parity rows now report MEASURED passes instead of "vacuous pass assumed"), and optional-content /Name + /AS checks (20). Reviewed for what the change could expose: nothing new reaches any output that isn't derived from the same qpdf JSON the parser already consumed; all new findings render through the existing escaped pipelines; unmapped-tag and RoleMap lists are capped and sorted; no new route, no schema change, nothing new collected or stored — data-retention policy unchanged at v1.14. **Calibration: all 27 control PDFs keep byte-identical scores, grades, category scores, and gate verdicts** — the new checks fire only on documents that actually carry the defects. The landing-page checklist promoted checkpoints 17/19 (engine-partial) and 20 (engine), pinned by test, and was rewritten in plain language for non-technical readers (why "Matterhorn", what veraPDF is, and a plain "no" to "does this change my score?"). Tests 2,619 → 2,650.


### v1.91.0 — 2026-08-25 · veraPDF absence is disclosed, never hidden — and the landing page discloses Matterhorn coverage checkpoint by checkpoint (presentation + one attach-rule change; no new attack surface)

A completeness audit of the analyzer against the Matterhorn Protocol (the PDF Association's PDF/UA test model — 31 checkpoints, 136 failure conditions) found the machine-check layer sound but its failure mode silent: when veraPDF did not run (unconfigured binary, saturated JVM queue), the report's PDF/UA panel simply disappeared, so a PDF report looked complete while the machine-checkable Matterhorn conditions went unexamined. The panel now renders for every PDF — live result, both views, stored shared reports — and says **"Did not run"** with a plain statement that not run means not checked, never passed; the API attaches `pdfUaVerdict` with `available: false` instead of omitting it, and `rebuild.sh` probes the veraPDF engine through `/status?format=json` after every deploy so a lost binary is loud at deploy time. The landing page gains the checkpoint-by-checkpoint coverage checklist (audit engine / veraPDF / human review per checkpoint). Reviewed for what the change could expose: the disclosure branch renders static repo-authored copy plus the configured veraPDF link only — no document-derived or request-derived value; the unavailable verdict shape carries no filesystem paths (the v1.38.0 generic-error discipline is unchanged); the checklist is a static data module rendered through ordinary interpolation; the deploy probe is a read-only GET against the already-public status JSON. No new route, no schema change, nothing new collected or stored; data-retention policy unchanged at v1.14. Overclaim guards are pinned by test: the human-judgment checkpoints (Flicker, Color and Contrast, Article Threads) can never claim machine coverage, and Character Mappings stays veraPDF-attributed until an in-house glyph census ships. Tests 2,602 → 2,619.


### v1.90.0 — 2026-08-25 · The re-audit summary counts public uploads only — fleet re-scans no longer drown the signal (semantic narrowing of a published figure; no new attack surface)

On its first live day, `document_progress_30d` read 3,781 documents / 3,293 re-audited / median lift 0: the trusted-tool fleet re-scans the same unchanged documents weekly, and its runs buried the human remediation story the block exists to tell. The grouping now counts `privileged = 0` rows only — fleet runs excluded, and unknown-tier rows from before migration 12 excluded with them (unknown might be the fleet), so the figures climb from when tier recording began, the same behavior `privileged_audits` has had since v1.86.0. Reviewed for what the change could expose: nothing — it *narrows* what is published; the SQL predicate is a constant, no new value reaches the payload or the HTML, and `distinct_documents` deliberately still counts every tier (a volume figure, contextualized by `privileged_audits`, now pinned by test). The card's caveat states the exclusion and the climb-from date in plain language; data-retention policy → v1.14 (§ 14 clarification), held to the code by `statusProgressPolicy.test.ts`. Two new exclusion tests: a leaked fleet row would flip `improvable` (watched directly), and unknown-tier rows contribute nothing. Tests 2,599 → 2,602.

### v1.89.1 — 2026-08-25 · The v1.89.0 What's New link to the status page works again (one config field; no new attack surface)

The v1.89.0 announcement linked to `/status?html` without `linkExternal: true`, so the banner and the What's New archive rendered a client-side `<NuxtLink>` to a Nitro server route — the Vue router shows its own "Page not found" without contacting the server, while the direct URL worked throughout. Reviewed for what the change could expose: nothing — one boolean on one announcement entry plus one test; no route, data, or retention change, and nothing new is served. The gap is now pinned as a data contract: the banner suite walks the real `ANNOUNCEMENTS` and fails if any entry pointing at `/status` lacks the flag (the component-honours-the-flag half was already pinned after v1.39.0 shipped the same class of bug). Tests 2,598 → 2,599.

### v1.89.0 — 2026-08-25 · `/status` shows whether documents improve — aggregate re-audit statistics (new public figures; nothing new collected; reviewed with one accepted residual)

Two new aggregate families on the public status document, in both representations: `distinct_documents` (distinct uploaded contents per window, counted by hash inside SQLite) and `document_progress_30d` (the audit → fix → re-audit loop grouped by file name inside SQLite — documents checked, re-checked, improved, reached an A, median score change). Reviewed for what the change could expose. No injection path: every new query builds its `IN` list from `?` placeholders bound to config constants, and the time bound is a bound integer. No disclosure path: the grouping SQL selects only numbers — the file name is the partition key and never appears in a SELECT list, the hash is consumed by `COUNT(DISTINCT …)` — and `statusPrivacy.test.ts` now seeds two versions of a secret filename and proves the new blocks consumed the name and both hashes while neither appears in the serialized JSON; the payload's top-level allow-list gained the two keys as deliberate additions. No script-injection path in the HTML card: only numbers reach the web tier, and every cell passes the same `escapeHtml` as the rest of the page. Below `STATUS.PROGRESS_MIN_DOCS` (5) re-checked documents the median is `null` and the HTML suppresses every rate, so no derived figure ever describes a single visitor's documents; raw counts stay published like every other aggregate. **Residual, accepted:** with no accounts, same-named uploads merge in the grouped counts, so someone who already knows a document's exact file name could infer its score movement by differencing counts during quiet traffic — it discloses only an accessibility score, and anyone holding the file can already obtain the exact score by auditing it; the same granularity class as the long-published per-window grade distributions. Nothing new is collected or stored — no schema change, no new route; data-retention policy → v1.13 (§ 14), its wording held to exactly what the code does by `statusProgressPolicy.test.ts`. Tests 2,586 → 2,598.

### v1.88.5 — 2026-08-25 · `logs.sh` shows the remediation loop: grouped `docs` view, per-document `doc` history; `tail` waits for the day's first error (operator tooling, no new attack surface)

Two additions to the operator script and one polish, all reading the same files as before. `./logs.sh docs` groups the activity rows by document and prints one line per file audited 2 or more times — runs, distinct versions (content hashes), first and last score, and the change between them, newest activity first — so the audit → fix → re-audit loop the activity log records is visible at a glance; `./logs.sh doc PATTERN` prints one document's every audit, oldest first, across every day on file, matched by file-name fragment or content-hash prefix. `./logs.sh tail` started before the day's first error now says so and waits for the file instead of surfacing tail's `cannot open` warning. Reviewed for what the change could expose: nothing — both views are re-arrangements of rows staff could already read in full, over the same `forge` SSH credential or directly on the server; no route, credential, column, or retention period changed, and nothing new is served by the site. `logsSh.test.ts` (+11 → 36) pins the grouping math, both window semantics, hash-prefix matching, the failed-audit exclusion, and the tail wait-then-follow handoff. Tests 2,575 → 2,586.

### v1.88.4 — 2026-08-23 · A bare `./logs.sh` shows 500 recent audits instead of 50 (operator tooling, no new attack surface)

One number in the operator script: the default view a bare `./logs.sh` prints grew from the 50 most recent audits to the 500 most recent, for more history per look. Reviewed for what the change could expose: nothing — the larger view draws on the same files, read over the same `forge` SSH credential or directly on the server; no route, credential, column, or retention period changed, and nothing new is served by the site. The default itself is now pinned: `logsSh.test.ts` renders a 505-row fixture day and holds a bare run to exactly the 500 newest rows (the previous fixture was too small to notice the constant regressing), and holds the help header to naming the real default. Tests 2,574 → 2,575.

### v1.88.3 — 2026-08-23 · `logs.sh` usable out of the box: recent audits by default, a help command, date examples (operator tooling, no new attack surface)

A developer-experience release for the operator script only. A bare `./logs.sh` now shows the 50 most recent audits across days instead of a file listing; `help` is a listed command that prints a rewritten header (quick start, both places to run it, what the files are, every command, the DATE shape with accepted and rejected examples, every format); `today` and `yesterday` are accepted as dates. Reviewed for what the change could expose: nothing — the script reads the same files over the same `forge` SSH credential, or directly on the server; nothing new is served by the site; no credential, route, column, or retention period changed. One correctness fix on the way: output is now collected before it is paged, and because bash disables `errexit` inside `$(…)`, every capturing assignment carries an explicit `|| exit $?` — a malformed date had printed a second, bogus error and, for `errors`, exited 0; `logsSh.test.ts` pins exactly one message and exit 1. Pre-existing posture re-verified. Tests 2,550 → 2,574.

### v1.88.2 — 2026-08-23 · Remediation job rows purge again — a foreign key had silently defeated the 30-day retention (retention fix, no new attack surface)

Found by the previous release's own sweep summary on its first production run: step 4 of the retention sweep had failed on every run with `FOREIGN KEY constraint failed`, because `remediation_events` referenced `remediation_jobs` while the events are kept seven years and the job rows only thirty days — so no finished job row had ever been deleted (62 on production, the oldest 97 days old). That is a retention promise in the data-retention policy (§ 7) that was not being kept, in the keep-too-long direction. Migration 15 rebuilds the events table without the constraint: the events are the standalone audit trail the policy describes and outlive their job by design; nothing is collected, served, or exposed differently, and no retention period changed — the existing 30-day window is simply enforced from this release on. Tests 2,545 → 2,550.

### v1.88.1 — 2026-08-23 · Log access script, sweep self-reporting, `created_at` index (ops follow-ups, no new attack surface)

Three follow-ups from the v1.88.0 review, none of them a vulnerability fix. `logs.sh` at the repository root is an operator tool: it reads the activity CSVs and the error log over the existing `forge` SSH credential (or directly on the server) and renders them as a table, Markdown, TSV, or onto the operator's own clipboard — nothing new is served by the site and no new credential exists. The retention sweep now logs a one-line self-report (counts and step names only) and sends captured step errors to stderr, so a step failing every five minutes is visible instead of silent. Migration 14 adds `idx_audit_created_at` — an index, no data change — so the export's per-day query, the retention purge and `/status`'s windowed counts stop scanning the table. README test tables now cover every test file; CI actions bumped to versions targeting the current runner Node. Pre-existing posture re-verified. Tests 2,534 → 2,545.

### v1.88.0 — 2026-08-22 · Failed audits recorded; daily activity export on the server (feature + ops, not a vulnerability fix)

Adds an auditor-facing record rather than fixing a flaw. An audit the tool attempted and could not complete now leaves its own `audit_log` row (`<type>-failed`, NULL score/grade/hash, a one-word reason from a closed set — never error text; migration 13), and the retention sweep writes one derived CSV per Chicago calendar day of that table to `logs/` at the application root on the server: the usage log's own fields and 365-day window, pruned by file-name date, not in backups, **not served by any route**. Reviewed for what it adds to the attack surface and to retention: no new route or parameter; the CSV writer quotes per RFC 4180 and neutralises formula injection (managers open these in Excel); files are `0600` in a `0700` directory owned by the service user; pruning deletes only names of the exact shape it writes. The new event types sit outside every `/status` counting allow-list, pinned by test. Two log-noise trims (page-audit navigation failures and 4xx responses log one line) write nothing that carries an IP, token, user agent or body — also pinned. Data-retention policy v1.12 describes both additions; the file name stays the one field that can carry personal information, and the policy says so. Also recorded: `pm2-logrotate` 3.0.0's compression setting is ineffective (documented, not worked around). The same release adds an application error log — a tee of the service's own stderr into `logs/errors-YYYY-MM-DD.log`, 30 days, 50 MB/day cap — reviewed on the same terms: it holds exactly what stderr already held (never the requester's address, browser identifier, token or request body; file names, page addresses, library paths and the addresses of servers the tool tried to reach can appear, as the policy states), on the same server, never served; and a conformance test pins the policy page's retention windows, directory and reason list to the code's constants. Tests 2,431 → 2,534.

### v1.87.1 — 2026-08-21 · "What's New" entries link to the pages they mention (copy/navigation, not a security change)

The landing-page security announcement referenced "the security log" and "section 10 of the data-retention policy" but gave no link. It now links **"Read the security review log" → `/data-retention#security-audits`** (§10, the on-site security review history — the same content this README's Security section mirrors technically). Two older status-related announcements gained **"View the status page" → `/status?html`** links. New standing practice: any `ANNOUNCEMENTS` entry that references a reachable page (data-retention policy, changelog, security log, status page, GitHub repo page) carries a working link. Conventions matched exactly — internal Vue-page links for on-site pages (the §10 link deep-links via the `#security-audits` anchor), and `linkExternal: true` + the `/status?html` form for the status page, which is a Nitro server route a plain link would 404. **No new attack surface:** three existing announcements gained `linkText`/`linkTo`; no new announcement, behavior, storage, retention, or dependency change. Tests 2,431 (unchanged).

### v1.87.0 — 2026-08-21 · App processes bind loopback in production, closing the `0.0.0.0` exposure (attack-surface reduction)

Closes the 2026-08-20 finding that the API (5103) and web (5102) listened on `0.0.0.0` — reachable on every interface with only the host firewall in front. Both now bind `127.0.0.1` in production: the API through a new pure `resolveBindHost(isProduction, DEPLOY.BIND_HOST)` helper, the web (Nitro) through a `HOST` env var in `ecosystem.config.cjs`. nginx already proxies to `127.0.0.1` and nothing legitimate hits the raw ports from off-host (the fleet uses `https://audit.icjia.app`), so the exposure is removed with zero functional change — **verified at the socket level**, a production start binds `127.0.0.1:5103` while a development start still binds all interfaces. **Production only by design:** dev keeps binding all interfaces because the Nuxt dev proxy targets `localhost:5103`, which resolves to IPv6 `::1` first on some systems, so an IPv4-only bind would break it intermittently — and the finding is about the production droplet. **Scoped to this app:** only `apps/api/src/index.ts` and this repo's `ecosystem.config.cjs` (which defines just `file-audit-api`/`file-audit-web`) change; other processes on the shared host (Strapi) run from their own configs and are untouched — no server-wide, nginx, or `/etc/environment` change. `bindLoopback.test.ts` pins that the API host and the web `HOST` agree on `DEPLOY.BIND_HOST` and that neither can silently regress to `0.0.0.0`. **Hardened.** Deploy picks up the new web `HOST` automatically (`rebuild.sh` restarts with `--update-env`); verify with `ss -lntp | grep -E '510[23]'` showing `127.0.0.1`. No new attack surface — it removes surface; no change to rate limits, SSRF controls, size caps, or dependencies. Tests 2,425 → 2,431.

### v1.86.0 — 2026-08-21 · Privileged-tier audit counts on /status; the day's server hardening recorded (feature + ops, not a vulnerability fix)

Adds a security-monitoring signal rather than fixing a flaw. `/status` now reports how much audit volume came through the `API_PRIVILEGED_TOKEN` (trusted-tool) tier versus the public tier — the measure you watch after rotating the single shared token to confirm its use matches the fleet and nothing else. It is backed by one new `audit_log` column (`privileged`, migration → user_version 12), nullable so pre-migration rows read as unknown rather than being fabricated as either tier. **This records a property of the shared service token, never an identity** — the "nothing about who" guarantee (v1.68.0) is unchanged, and `statusPrivacy.test.ts`'s payload allow-list was extended deliberately, not incidentally. `recordAudit` now requires the tier as an explicit field so a new call site is caught by the compiler, not discovered later mis-recording; all eight call sites were threaded with their real tier. Data-retention policy → v1.11 (§ 8a documents the column, § 14 the change). **New / Note.** This release is also the recorded home for the **operational server hardening of 2026-08-21** — the privileged-token rotation, database and backup permission tightening, removal of an unused print service, and OS patch — disclosed in § 10 of the data-retention policy; it rode this release rather than forcing a deploy of its own, as planned. **OPS.** No new external surface, and no change to rate limits, SSRF controls, size caps, or dependencies. Tests 2,415 → 2,425.

### v1.85.0 — 2026-08-20 · Nuxt 4.5.2 + dependency pins — six framework advisories closed, one reachable in production (SECURITY RELEASE)

The first release in this log that closes a hole which was demonstrably reachable rather than theoretical. The app defines no server islands, yet `/__nuxt_island/<name>` answered on production with 204, carrying this site's own CSP nonce and security headers — while sibling paths 404'd with a different, minimal CSP, confirming the handler was registered and executing. On Nuxt 4.4.7 that endpoint carries **server-side RCE via runtime template injection in island props**, an unauthenticated OOM via unbounded `v-for`, and unauthenticated CPU exhaustion from hashing the island body before validating it. **Fixed by nuxt → 4.5.2**, which also clears unauthorized component instantiation, a runtime payload cache able to disclose one visitor's SSR data to another (`>=4.4.0 <=4.5.0` — 4.4.7 was inside that range), and mixed-case route-rule dropping.
